This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project to make the world’s books discoverable online.

It has survived long enough for the copyright to expire and for the book to enter the public domain. A public domain book is one that was never subject to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary from country to country. Public domain books are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture, and knowledge that is often difficult to discover.

Marks, notations, and other marginalia present in the original volume will appear in this file—a reminder of this book’s long journey from the publisher to a library and finally to you.

### Usage guidelines

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the public, and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive; therefore, in order to keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying.

We also ask that you:

*   **Make non-commercial use of the files:** We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for personal, non-commercial purposes.
*   **Refrain from automated querying:** Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google’s system. If you are conducting research on machine translation, optical character recognition, or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help.
*   **Maintain attribution:** The Google “watermark” you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it.
*   **Keep it legal:** Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, the work is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still under copyright varies from country to country, and we cannot offer guidance on whether any specific use of any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book’s appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liability can be quite severe.

Google’s mission is to organize the world’s information and to make it universally accessible and useful. --- Page 14 ---
<meta>This is the beginning of the preface for a modernized edition of George Gifford's work on witchcraft.</meta>

# PREFACE.

---

**GEORGE GIFFORD** was a very notable preacher of his time. An account of him and his publications can be found in Wood’s *Athenae Oxonienses* <note>original: “Athenæ.” This is a biographical dictionary of Oxford writers and bishops compiled by Anthony Wood in the 17th century.</note>. At Maldon, in Essex, he was located in the middle of a superstitious district, famous both then and afterward in the history of witchcraft. As early as 1587, Gifford published *A Discourse of the Subtle Practices of Devils by Witches and Sorcerers* <note>original: “A Discourse of the subtill Practises of Devilles by Witches and Sorcerers.”</note>. Six years later, in 1593, the first edition of the present dialogue appeared, of which a second edition was printed in 1603. This later version has been used as the text for the present edition.

This dialogue was thought to be worthy of reprinting, both as an excellent example of the everyday language of the reign of Queen Elizabeth I, and for the good sense with which the writer treats a subject on which so many people lost their reason, and the curious... <note>The text ends mid-sentence on this page.</note>

<summary>This preface introduces George Gifford, a prominent Elizabethan preacher and author from Essex, highlighting his 16th-century writings on witchcraft and their significance as historical and linguistic records.</summary>
<keywords>George Gifford, witchcraft, Maldon, Essex, Elizabethan era, Anthony Wood, skepticism, demonology</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface, concluding the discussion on the historical context of George Gifford's work and its relationship to the views of King James I.</meta>

allusions which it contains to the superstitions of that age. It is remarkable that the second edition appeared in the opening year of the reign of a monarch <note>King James I (reigned 1603–1625), who published "Daemonologie" in 1597.</note>, who published a treatise on witchcraft that was as much characterized by bigoted ignorance as the present work is by enlightened views. Still, even George Gifford was either not entirely free from the superstitious beliefs of his age, or he was afraid to state his opinions to their full extent; for many of his arguments against the activities <note>original: "operations"</note> of witches need to be carried out only a very little further to disprove the existence of the witches themselves.

<sig>W.</sig>

<summary>This page concludes the preface's introduction, contrasting Gifford's relatively enlightened skepticism about witchcraft with the superstitious "bigoted ignorance" of King James I. It suggests that Gifford’s logic almost leads to a total denial of witchcraft, though he stopped short of that conclusion.</summary>
<keywords>George Gifford, James I, witchcraft, superstition, skepticism, Daemonologie, 1603</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues from the front matter of George Gifford's 1603 edition of "A Dialogue concerning Witches and Witchcrafts." While the previous page was illegible, this page begins the formal dedication to Robert Clarke.</meta>

# TO THE RIGHT WORSHIPFUL MASTER
# ROBERT CLARKE, ONE OF HER MAJESTY’S
# BARONS OF HER HIGHNESS’S COURT
# OF EXCHEQUER.

---

Several years ago, right worshipful <note>A formal title of respect used for someone of high social or legal standing.</note>, I published a small treatise concerning witches to reveal some of Satan's tricks and subtle practices, lest the more ignorant people should be led astray and seduced more and more by them. The errors are far more gross, and the sins much greater—into which he seduces multitudes by means of witches—than they are commonly thought to be. It happens in many places, quite suddenly as it seems to me and no doubt by the heavy judgment of God, that the devils, as if let loose, prevail more than I have ever heard of before. For when men have treated the hearing of God's voice so lightly—refusing to be instructed by Him—they are justly handed over to be taught by devils and to learn their ways. Satan is now heard speaking and is believed. He speaks through conjurers <term>magicians who claim to call up spirits through rituals</term>, through sorcerers, and through witches, and his word is accepted. He devises a number of things to be done, and they are put into practice and followed. The high providence of God Almighty and His sovereign rule over

<summary>In this dedication, George Gifford addresses Judge Robert Clarke, warning that society's neglect of God's word has allowed Satan to increase his influence through witchcraft and sorcery.</summary>
<keywords>George Gifford, Robert Clarke, Court of Exchequer, witchcraft, Satan, divine judgment, spiritual deception</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the formal dedication to Robert Clarke from George Gifford's 1603 edition of "A Dialogue concerning Witches and Witchcrafts." Having just established God's sovereign rule, Gifford now explains how the devil deceives humans within the limits of that divine power.</meta>

Everything is explained to us in the Scriptures in such a way that without Him, not even a sparrow can fall to the ground. All the hairs of our head are numbered. The devils would love to hurt and destroy both men and animals, along with other creatures, with bodily harm. However, all the devils in hell are so chained up and restrained by this high providence that they cannot even pluck a wing from one poor little wren without special permission given to them by the ruler of the whole earth. 

And yet, the witches are made to believe that at their request—and to please them by acting out their anger—their spirits lame and kill both people and animals. Then, to spread this belief among the public, these subtle spirits betray them <note>original: "bewray them"</note> and make them openly confess that they have done such great things—things which all the devils together could never do at any human's request. For if they were able, they would not wait to be asked. 

God sometimes gives the devil power to afflict both people and animals with physical harm. If he can, he will do it while pretending to be requested and sent by witches. But for us to imagine either that the witches' "sending" him gives him power, or that he would not do what God has already allowed him to do unless they requested it, is completely absurd. 

There are many diseases in the bodies of humans and animals that the devil sees will lead to lameness or death; he then tricks the witches into believing <note>original: "beareth the witches in hand"</note> that he is the one causing them. He works through his other kind of witches—whom the people call "cunning men" and "wise women" <term>folk healers or diviners who used charms and magic to 'help' others</term>—to confirm all his schemes. By having them teach many remedies, he ensures that he is sought after and honored as if he were God.

<summary>Gifford argues that God's absolute sovereignty limits the devil's power, explaining that witches are merely deceived into believing they control spirits who are actually acting under God's permission.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Providence, Satanic deception, George Gifford, witches, cunning men, wise women, spiritual delusion</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page concludes the formal dedication to Robert Clarke from George Gifford's 1603 edition of "A Dialogue concerning Witches and Witchcrafts." Gifford transitions from explaining the theology of the devil's deception to stating his purpose for writing this book: to prevent the execution of innocent people.</meta>

These beliefs take root in the hearts of the people, making them afraid of witches and raising suspicions and rumors against various innocent people. As a result, many guiltless people are condemned to death based on men's oaths, and much innocent blood is shed. How subtly he <note>the devil</note> devises <note>original: "contriveth"</note> these matters, I have explained to the best of my small ability in this short treatise. I have written it in the form of a dialogue to make it more suitable for the understanding <note>original: "capacity"</note> of the common people <note>original: "simpler sort"</note>.

I am bold enough to offer it to your worship—not because you need to be taught these things, as you are zealously devoted to the Gospel and so well-grounded in faith in God’s supreme providence. Indeed, I have been delighted to hear and see the wise and godly approach your worship takes on the seat of justice when such people have been arraigned <term>called to court to answer criminal charges</term>. Therefore, I offer it as a testimony of my grateful mind for the favors and kindness you have shown toward me, and I entreat your worship to accept it. If it can do good for any of those with less knowledge, I shall be glad. If I have made any errors, once they are pointed out to me, I will be ready to correct them.

->At your worship's command in all duties,<-

->GEORGE GIFFARD.<-

<summary>George Gifford concludes his dedication to Robert Clarke, explaining that he wrote this dialogue to educate the common people and prevent the execution of innocent people falsely accused of witchcraft. He praises Clarke's wisdom as a judge and offers the work as a token of gratitude for Clarke's past kindness.</summary>
<keywords>George Gifford, Robert Clarke, witchcraft trials, innocent blood, judicial wisdom, religious providence, dialogue format, legal justice</keywords>

<meta>This page introduces the characters and begins a dialogue between Samuel and Daniel regarding Samuel's troubled state of mind.</meta>

### THE SPEAKERS.

->*Samuel. Daniel. The wife of Samuel. M. B.*<-
->*schoolmaster. The Goodwife R.*<-

---

*Samuel.* It is good to meet you, old friend. I am glad to see you looking so well. How are all our good friends in your part of the country?

*Daniel.* I trust they are all in good health; they were when I left home. I am sorry to see you looking so pale. Have you been sick lately?

*Samuel.* Truly, no. I thank God I have been in fairly good health, but it seems to me that my food <note>original: "meate"; in this period, "meat" referred to food in general, not just animal flesh</note> has not been doing me any good lately.

*Daniel.* What is the matter, man? Are you worrying too much about worldly things? Take heed of that, for the Scripture says, "Worldly sorrow produces death" (2 Corinthians 7:10). It is a great sin arising from unbelief and distrust in God’s providence <note>God's protective care and spiritual direction</note> when men are overly anxious about the world.

*Samuel.* Indeed, my mind is troubled, but not for what you suggest, for I hope in God that I shall not lack for anything as long as I live.

*Daniel.* Is it some trouble of conscience regarding sin? If it is, that may turn out for the best.

<vocab>Samuel, Daniel, schoolmaster, goodwife, worldly sorrow, 2 Corinthians 7:10, unbelief, providence</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces the participants of the dialogue and begins a conversation between Samuel and Daniel, where Samuel admits to being deeply troubled despite having no physical illness or financial worries.</summary>
<keywords>dialogue, Samuel, Daniel, spiritual health, providence, worldly sorrow, anxiety</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Samuel and Daniel, shifting from general concerns about health and worry to Samuel's specific fear of witchcraft in his community.</meta>

# A DIALOGUE CONCERNING

*Samuel.* Oh no, no, I know of no reason why.

*Daniel.* Why, what is it then? If I may be so bold, please tell me. I believe you consider me your friend.

*Samuel.* Indeed, I have always found you to be my very good friend, and I am sure you will give me the best advice <note>original: "counsell"</note> you can. Truly, we live here in a bad part of the country—I think it is even one of the worst in England.

*Daniel.* Is that so? I think you live in a fine area, with sweet, wholesome air and fertile <note>original: "fruitfull"</note> grounds.

*Samuel.* The air, man! I find no fault with the air; there are wicked <note>original: "naughtie"; in the 16th century, this meant wicked or morally bad, rather than just mischievous</note> people here.

*Daniel.* Wicked people! Where can a man live and not find them? Swearers, liars, abusers <note>original: "railers"; those who use insulting or harsh language</note>, slanderers, drunkards, adulterers, rowdy people, spendthrifts <note>original: "unthrifts"</note>, gamblers <note>original: "dicers"</note>, and proud, arrogant people are found everywhere in great abundance.

*Samuel.* No, I do not mean them; I do not care about them. These witches—these ugly <note>original: "evill favoured"</note>, old witches—they trouble me.

*Daniel.* What, do you believe yourself to be bewitched?

*Samuel.* No, no, I trust that no evil spirit can hurt me, but I hear of much harm done by them. They lame men and kill their cattle; yes, they even destroy both men and children. They say there is scarcely any town or village in this entire county <note>original: "shire"</note> that does not have at least one or two witches in it. In truth <note>original: "In good sooth"</note>, I may tell you as a friend: when I go into my enclosed fields <note>original: "closes"</note>, I am afraid. For now and then I see a hare, and my gut tells me <note>original: "my conscience giveth me"</note> it is a witch or some witch's spirit <note>a familiar, or a demonic entity in animal form</note>, because she stares at me so intently. And sometimes I see an ugly weasel...

<summary>Samuel reveals that his distress stems from a deep-seated fear of local witches, whom he believes are responsible for harming people and livestock throughout the county. He confesses to being terrified by animals like hares and weasels, suspecting them of being witches in disguise or their supernatural spirits.</summary>
<keywords>witchcraft, folklore, familiars, superstition, rural life, Samuel, Daniel, England, spirits</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Samuel and Daniel. Samuel is describing the suspicious events and livestock deaths that have led him to fear he is being targeted by a witch.</meta>

# WITCHES AND WITCHCRAFTS.

...run through my yard, and there is a foul, large cat sometimes in my barn, which I have no liking for.

*Daniel.* You have never had any harm done to you yet by any witch, have you?

*Samuel.* Trust me, I cannot tell, but I fear I have; for there are two or three in our town whom I do not like, especially one old woman. I have been as careful to please her as I ever was to please my own mother, and I give her one thing or another every now and then; yet it seems to me <note>original: "methinkes"</note> she frowns at me every so often. And I had a hog that ate its food with the others and seemed very well to us overnight, but in the morning it was stone-dead <note>original: "starke dead"</note>. My wife has even had five or six hens die recently. Some of my neighbors advise me to burn something alive, like a hen or a hog <note>This was a common folk remedy; it was believed that burning a bewitched animal would compel the witch to appear or break the spell</note>. Others want me to seek help from a <term>cunning man</term> <note>A "white witch" or folk healer believed to have the power to identify witches and undo their spells</note> in time, before I suffer any further harm. I would be glad to do what is best.

*Daniel.* Is there any <term>cunning man</term> nearby who helps people?

*Samuel.* There is one, they say, about twenty miles from here at T. B., who has helped many. And I know this much: there was an acquaintance of mine only two miles from here who suffered great losses; he lost two or three cows <note>original: "kine"</note>, six hogs—he wouldn't have taken fifteen shillings a hog for them—and a mare. He went to that same man and told him he suspected an old woman in the parish. And I think he told me that the man showed her to him in a mirror <note>original: "glasse"</note> and told him she had three or four <term>imps</term> <note>original: "impes"; small demons or spirit familiars believed to serve a witch</note>, which some call <term>puckrels</term> <note>A regional English term for a small familiar spirit or imp</note>, one like a gray cat,

<summary>Samuel details his suspicions of a local woman following the mysterious deaths of his livestock and mentions seeking help from a "cunning man" who uses magic mirrors to identify witches.</summary>
<keywords>witchcraft, livestock death, cunning man, imps, puckrels, folk magic, familiar spirits</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the conversation between Samuel and Daniel. Samuel is recounting success stories of local "cunning" people who have successfully identified witches and cured supernatural ailments.</meta>

# A DIALOGUE CONCERNING

...another like a weasel, another like a mouse. "May a vengeance take them," he said. It is a great pity the country is not rid of them! And the man told him also what he should do. That was half a year ago, and he has never suffered any harm since. 

There is also a woman at R. H., twenty-five miles from here, who has a great reputation, and people flock to her daily. A neighbor of mine had a child—a ten-year-old girl—who became lame. She had such a pain in her back that she could not sit upright. He went to that woman, and she told him that he had some bad neighbor and that the child was <term>forespoken</term> <note>original: "fores poken," meaning bewitched or cursed by someone's speech</note>, as she suspected. Indeed <note>original: "marie," a common exclamation derived from "by Mary"</note>, she said if he would go home and bring her some of the clothes the child slept in all night, she would tell him for certain. He went home and put a table napkin around the girl's neck all night; in the morning, he took it with him, and she told him the girl was bewitched indeed. She told him what he should do, and he found a remedy. The girl is well today—a pretty, <note>original: "quicke," meaning lively or spirited</note> girl. 

Another of my neighbors had a wife who was much troubled, and he went to her. She told him his wife was haunted by a fairy. I cannot tell what she commanded him to do, but the woman is happy <note>original: "merrie"</note> at this hour. I have heard—though I dare not say it is so—that she wears St. John's Gospel <note>It was a common folk practice to wear a written portion of the Gospel of John as a protective amulet against evil spirits</note>, or some part of it, on her person.

**Daniel.** If you have such <term>cunning men</term> <note>Folk healers or "white witches" believed to have the power to combat malevolent magic</note> and women, why do you need to be so afraid?

**Samuel.** Alas, man, I am ready to go <note>original: "teeme it to go," meaning he is willing or can find the heart to make the journey</note>. Some counsel me to go to the man at T. B., and some to the woman at R. H. Between the two of them, I have delayed <note>original: "lingred the time"</note>, and I fear I may be ruined before I get...

<summary>Samuel shares stories of neighbors who were helped by "cunning" folk at T. B. and R. H. to cure bewitchments and fairy hauntings, leading Daniel to ask why Samuel hasn't sought help yet.</summary>
<keywords>cunning man, cunning woman, forespoken, bewitchment, fairy haunting, St. John's Gospel charm, folk medicine</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the conversation between Samuel and Daniel from George Gifford's 1593 work on witchcraft. Samuel has just finished describing how various neighbors found remedies for witchcraft through "cunning folk," and he is now weighing his own options for dealing with a suspected witch.</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>WITCHES AND WITCHCRAFTS.</header>

remedy. Some advise me to beat and scratch <note>original: "claw"</note> the witch until I draw blood from her <note>A common folk belief held that "scratching" a witch to draw blood would break her spell and provide protection to the victim</note>, and to threaten her that I will have her hanged. If I knew which course was best, I would do it.

**Daniel.** I see your danger is that you are caught between two stools <note>An idiom meaning to be caught in a dilemma between two choices, risking failure by failing to commit to either</note>.

**Samuel.** That is very true. If I had heard of only one, I would have gone before now, and I am glad that I met with you. I pray you, give me your best advice; I trust you wish me well.

**Daniel.** Truly, I will give you the best advice I can, which I am sure will do you good if you will follow it; for indeed, I pity your situation. It is most certain that you are bewitched.

**Samuel.** Bewitched? Do you think I am bewitched? I feel no harm in my body; you make me more afraid.

**Daniel.** No, I do not think that the old woman has bewitched you, or that your body is bewitched, but that the devil has bewitched your mind with blindness and unbelief. He does this to draw you away from God, even to worship the devil himself by seeking help from demons <note>original: "divels"</note>. It is a pitiful <note>original: "lamentable"</note> case to see how the devil has bewitched thousands these days, causing them to run after him and even to offer sacrifice to him.

This was shown by the enchanters before Pharaoh, when their rods appeared to be turned into serpents (Exodus 7). Therefore, one devil can seem to be four or five, and four or five can seem to be one. It is, therefore, merely the craft of Satan to make a show of more or fewer.

*M. B.* Well, I do not dislike all this; I pray you, proceed.

*Dan.* Then further, notice well how the holy Scriptures depict the devils as mighty, terrible spirits, full of power, rage, and cruelty. ...and ease your shoulders, and let us reason the matter a little further. I pray you, let me ask you this question: Does the witch or the devil do the harm to men and cattle?

*M. B.* It is indeed a question worth asking; for undoubtedly the devil has no power until it is given to him to touch any creature, or to hurt or destroy the body, but only to tempt and lead people into sin. I am also sure that the witch cannot give him power, but only God above.

*Dan.* Put these two things together then: that the devil alone does the hurting, and that no one can give him power—neither man nor woman—but only God. Now tell me...

It is as if they had no foul sins or unbelief, or as if there did not remain a just, revenging God to punish them, or as if He did not still have the devils as the executioners of His wrath.

*Daniel.* When I say that God alone, and not the witches, gives power to the devils to plague and torment, it is so evident that I suppose one would hardly meet any person so ignorant <note>original: "grosse"</note> that they would not confess it to be true. In reality, when the Devil is given power to hurt, or when he knows that death or grievous diseases will naturally follow in either man or beast, he puts the witch into a rage and moves her to "send" him.

As I said before, here is the deep craft of Satan: he desires to be "sent" by witches, whereas, in fact, God has deployed him, seeing that no one can send him but God. Again, we must consider that there are natural causes in the bodies of men and beasts for grievous torments and diseases—yes, even causes of death. These cannot be so secret that the devil does not know them, even knowing when they are likely to take effect. Then he works upon the witch and sets her in a fury, and she "sends" him. Right as he is "sent," the man or the beast is suddenly and strangely tormented, falls lame, or dies. Then the witch is suspected, examined, and confesses...

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>Daniel continues his explanation from the previous page, shifting the focus from the devil's deceptions to why God allows such evils to occur. He argues that the presence of witches is a form of divine judgment on a society that has rejected God's truth.</meta>

<page-num>36</page-num>

<header>A DIALOGUE CONCERNING</header>

**Dan.** The craftiness of devils is so great that without the light of God's word, even the wisest men on earth would inevitably be deceived by them. We see that some men are so deeply involved in subtleties and can keep their schemes so hidden that others cannot detect them. How much more so the devils, who are exceedingly subtle and craftier than even the shrewdest men? 

The question you ask is, in my judgment, somewhat difficult. However, it is certain that if the Lord God more frequently gives the devils power to cause harm through witches—meaning, because the devils operate through such instruments—it is as a severe judgment <note>original: "heavy judgement"</note> against the wickedness of the people who despise the true and heavenly light of his word. 

As St. Paul shows—while prophesying the coming of the great Antichrist—because men did not receive the love of the truth, God gave the devil power (through the Antichrist and his ministers) to deceive them with lying signs and wonders <note>This refers to 2 Thessalonians 2:9-11, a common text used by Reformation-era writers to explain why God permits supernatural deception.</note>. Indeed, I would not say the devil is given power because of the witch herself, but rather because of the wickedness of the people. They deserve for the devil to have the power to deceive them further through these witches. 

Yet here we must be careful of a common error that many people rush into: they fail to see that God is provoked by their own sins to allow the devil to use such instruments. Instead, they focus their rage against the witch, as if she were the one in total control.

**M. B.** Surely, I would be a wretch <note>In this context, meaning a miserable or foolish person.</note> to deny that God gives the devils power to plague and deceive people because of their wickedness. But still, I would like to know...

...whether a godly, faithful man or woman may not be bewitched? We see that the devil was given power over Job.

**Dan.** This example of Job is not appropriate <note>original: "fit"</note> to prove that a godly person can be bewitched, since the devil is not said to have acted through witches against him. However, it does prove that not only the godly, but even the most godly (such as holy Job, who had no equal on earth), may be given into the hands of Satan to be afflicted and tempted for the sake of their trial. 

And, as I said, when God grants Satan the power to strike any of the godly with bodily plagues to test their faith and patience, the devil will desire—if he can manage it—to be sent by some witch and to make it known that he was sent. But the faithful must look away from the witch and deal directly with God, for the matter comes from him. 

When they have been tested, the Lord, in his own good time, will deliver those who depend on him, to their great praise and glory, just like valiant soldiers. It is, therefore, of no great importance <note>original: "force"</note> whether Satan comes against the godly through a witch, or whether he has no witch to use: if you overcome the Devil, you overcome everything. 

Indeed, he prevails greatly among the more ignorant people when he afflicts those who embrace the living <note>original: "lively"</note> word, making it seem as if the affliction was sent by a witch. For many people today actually quake and tremble, and their faith staggers. "If he has power," they think, "over those who are knowledgeable <note>original: "cunning"</note> in the Scriptures, then what good is their profession of faith? The witch is on their bones just as much as anyone else's." By this, it might seem, and...

# WITCHES AND WITCHCRAFTS.

...all your talk, and I cannot quite understand the last points you discussed. With your permission, M. B., I would like to ask two or three questions of my friend. 

About seven miles from here, at W. H., there was a man named M. He was quite wealthy and well-regarded by his neighbors. He pined away with sickness for half a year and finally died. After he was dead, his wife suspected foul play. She went to a cunning man <term>cunning man: a local practitioner of "white magic" or folk medicine, often sought to identify thieves or witches</term>—I do not know where—and desired to know what her husband died of. He told her that her husband died of witchcraft. He asked her if she suspected anyone in that area. She said there was one woman she did not like, a certain Mother W. <term>Mother: a common title for an elderly woman of lower social standing, often used for those accused of witchcraft</term>; her husband and this woman had a falling out, and he fell sick within two days afterward and never recovered. 

The cunning man showed her the woman as clearly in a glass <note>original: "glasse"; a mirror or crystal used for divination (scrying)</note> as we see one another, wearing the exact same clothes she was wearing at that very hour. She wore an old red cornered cap, such as women used to wear, and she appeared in the glass wearing that. He taught the wife how she might bring the woman to confess. 

Well, she followed his advice, went home, and had the woman arrested and brought before a Justice of the Peace. He examined her so wisely that in the end, she confessed she had killed the man. She was sent to prison, put on trial, condemned, and executed. Upon the ladder <note>the ladder leading to the gallows for hanging</note>, she seemed very penitent, asking all the world to forgive her. She said she had a spirit in the likeness of a yellow dun cat <note>original: "yellow dun cat"; "dun" refers to a grayish-brown or sandy color. This "familiar spirit" was believed to be a demon in animal form</note>. This cat came to her, she said, as she sat by her fire after she had fallen out with a neighbor of hers, and it wished that the vengeance of—

*Sam.* I am fully persuaded that he rules her heart.

*Dan.* Then she was his drudge <note>original: "drudge" — a person made to do hard, menial, or menial work; a slave</note>, and not he her servant. He does not need to be hired or entreated; for if her heart were set on sending him anywhere to those he knows he cannot hurt—or if he sees no way to make it *look* like he is hurting them—he can quickly turn her heart away from that. Well, the cat goes and kills the man, certain hogs, and a cow: how could she tell that the cat did it?

*Sam.* How could she tell? Why, he told her, man! And she saw and heard that the neighbor lost his livestock.

*Dan.* The cat would lie, would she not? For they say such cats are liars.

*Sam.* I do not trust the cat's words alone, but I believe it because the events turned out exactly as said.

*Dan.* Because the hogs and the cow died, are you sure the cat killed them? Might they not have died of some natural causes? For you see that both men and beasts can be well one moment and die suddenly.

*Sam.* That would be strange, if they should die of natural causes and have it happen so perfectly at the time after he was sent.

*Dan.* It is not strange at all; pay attention to what I tell you, and you shall easily see why. There are natural causes for pain and suffering, for lameness, and for death in the bodies of men and of beasts which lie so hidden and secret that the most learned physicians cannot detect them. But the devil sees them and can guess very closely the time when they will take effect. Then he works at it <note>original: "plie it"</note> to arrange things so that it may seem like he did it. If he has any witch to work through, he stirs up some occasion to make her fly into a rage with that person; and then he will ask to be sent, and tells her he is doing the harm. If he has no witch to work through, he will still start a conflict between that person and someone else whom he can bring under suspicion, as his greatest desire is to have innocent blood shed.

*Sam.* This is a complicated scheme indeed; how could the cat do all of this?

*Dan.* I told you before that the devils work together and can quickly and most craftily accomplish <term>original: "compasse" — to achieve or bring about through skill or craft</term> things which are far beyond the reach of human understanding. But sometimes the devil is given power to afflict and do harm. Even if we admit he was given power and did kill this man's livestock <note>original: "cattell"</note>, let us consider this: who do you think gave him the power to strike and kill? Did the witch give him the power, or the Lord God?

*Sam.* No, surely the witch cannot give him power.

He did so, and the woman told him that his child was bewitched; she warned that if he did not seek a remedy in time, the child would be lost. She told him to take some old clothes, let the child sleep in them all night, and then burn them. He would be able to see the truth by the burning: if they burned black, it proved the child was bewitched. Furthermore, she said that the witch would undoubtedly come there during the process. 

He followed her advice, and as sure as we are here, an old woman whom he suspected came in while they were burning the clothes, making an excuse for her visit. ...think a man may lawfully seek help from the hands of the devil?

***M. B.*** Some are persuaded that they do not seek help from the hand of devils when they go to the <term>wise men</term><note>original: "wise men"—folk healers or "cunning men" believed to possess supernatural knowledge or the power to break spells</note>, but that it is a gift which God has given them, specifically to do good with.

***Dan.*** I truly think that many of the people are so persuaded. But what reason is there for it? Does God, by His Spirit, tell where a thing is that is lost or stolen? Is it an angel from heaven, or the soul of some man who is dead, which appears in the crystal or in the <term>glass</term><note>original: "glasse"—a scrying mirror or reflective surface used to see visions of the past or future</note>, and shows the image of the party who has stolen, or who is a witch?

***M. B.*** I would rather hear what you think regarding these things than explain what I have thought.

***Dan.*** The devils made the heathen people believe that they were gods, and so ensured that they should worship them with divine worship. Through their craftiness, they had many ways to establish this. They entered into images <note>statues or idols</note> and gave answers out of them when they were questioned. In this, they used great craft; for since they could not tell what would actually happen, they phrased the <term>oracle</term><note>A priest or priestess acting as a medium through whom advice or prophecy was sought from the gods</note> in such a way that it was ambiguous and could be taken both ways. And so, whichever way it turned out, that seemed to be—and was taken to be—the meaning of the gods. It is not the speaking of good words or the wearing of some part of the Scriptures that defends against devils; therein lies the craftiness of Satan: to have those holy things so foully abused, and to make men put their trust in spoken words and sentences. Instead, the devils are resisted only by the power of faith, when the Holy Scriptures are written in the heart...

# WITCHES AND WITCHCRAFTS.

He told the man to prepare a breakfast and he would bring this friend. 

Then, this was the cure: he made the man strip naked and stand by a warm fire. Even though the man was already fat enough, the friend basted him all over with butter in front of the fire and made him wear a <term>sleek stone</term> <note>original: "sleeke stone"; a smooth, polished stone usually used for smoothing linen, here used as a fake medical charm.</note> against his skin under his belly. The man received an immediate remedy and later gave the friend great thanks.

*M. B.* I know men have many foolish imaginations. But even though one imagination may drive out another—which is not actually curing a disease, but merely curing an imagination—it does not follow that when there is an obvious <term>grief</term> <note>original: "griefe"; physical pain, illness, or bodily suffering.</note>, a man's mental conviction can help to cure it.

*Dan.* Yes, imagination does a great deal, even when there is an obvious disease. A man fears he is bewitched; this troubles all the powers of his mind, and that upsets the balance of his body, causes great physical changes, and brings about various pains. Now, when his mind is freed from such imaginations, the bodily suffering that grew from them is eased. Many of Satan’s "cures" are nothing more than this.

The woman had such confidence in the thing, and was so happy and glad, that she stopped weeping (for her frequent weeping and tears had ruined her eyes), and so, little by little, the moisture ceased and her eyes were healed. It happened that she lost the note, which caused her such grief, sorrow, and weeping that her eyes became sore again. Another person found the writing, opened it, and read it. It was written in the German language, to this effect, translated into English: 

—*May the devil pluck out your eyes and fill the holes with his dung.* 

Do you not think this was a fine <term>salve</term> <note>original: "salve"; a healing ointment or remedy.</note> to cure her eyes? If this medicine had taken its literal effect, her eyes would not...

# WITCHES AND WITCHCRAFTS.

*Dan.* The holy Scriptures do command that witches should be put to death: in that, you are right. ought to die, read first in the 22nd chapter of Exodus, verse 18, and there it is said, *You shall not permit a witch to live.*

*M. B.* We take that passage to refer to those witches who send their spirits to do harm; the others are not called witches.

*Dan.* It is that specific type of witch who is commanded there to be put to death, who is called a sorceress <note>original: "Mecasshephah," a Hebrew term often translated as "witch" or "sorceress" in the Old Testament</note>. Also, in the same place, when He says, "Let there not be found among you any such person," as He lists them there, He does not only mean that no one should practice such things, but also that they should be rooted out.

*M. B.* I must agree with what the word of God sets down. But this is the hardest matter of all: how they shall be convicted.

*Dan.* Why do you think it is the hardest matter to convict a witch? How is a thief or a murderer convicted except by proof? If there is strong <note>original: "vehement"</note> suspicion, and the party confesses the deed <note>original: "fact"</note> upon examination, that is sufficient proof. If the party denies it, and two or three people of good reputation <note>original: "credit"</note> testify under a solemn oath, based on their own knowledge, that the person is guilty of the deed, that is also sufficient proof. And touch—

**M. B.** That is the very reason why it is thought the devil would by no means want witches to be betrayed <note>original: "bewrayed"; revealed or exposed</note>, because he would prefer to lurk secretly to do mischief.

**Dan.** Indeed, that would be a good reason to prove your point if Satan received his power from the witch, or could do nothing except by her command <note>original: "sending"</note>, or needed to be sheltered by her, or had no desire to interfere unless he were hired to satisfy her wrath. How could he induce so many to use wicked and blasphemous charms and sorceries, and to abuse the blessed name of God and His most sacred word in such a horrible manner? *Dan.* It is the most insufficient proof that can be; for although he does tell the truth in exposing <note>original: "bewraying"; revealing or betraying</note> many, as their own confessions witness, yet he does it with an evil purpose. *Dan.* I believe that ought not to be done, for consider this comparison: a prince is displeased with a subject for some disloyalty, and an officer is sent from the prince to arrest <note>original: "attach"; a legal term for seizing a person or property by authority of a writ</note> and imprison him. Shall he or anyone else command this officer in the prince's name to let him alone and not to interfere? Is not their only path to pacify the prince, so that the prince himself will command the officer to stop? Even so, where God sends Satan as his executioner, the only way is to entreat the Lord to be pacified, for then the tormentor shall no longer remain.

*M. B.* How does what you say agree with what we read in the Acts of the Apostles, where Saint Paul commanded the devil to come out of a girl at Philippi <note>original: "mayde at Philippos"; referring to the slave girl with a spirit of divination mentioned in Acts 16:16-18</note>?

*Dan.* The holy apostles and others in the early Church <note>original: "primitive Church"</note> were given an extraordinary power to cast out devils and to heal diseases, and they exercised that same power by the direction and guidance <note>original: "instinct"</note> of the Holy Spirit. We may not use that as a pattern <note>original: "patterne"; meaning a precedent or rule to follow</note> for ourselves.

*M. B.* We see that devils are sometimes expelled.

*Dan.* They are expelled when the Lord is entreated; otherwise, they only *seem* to be bound by command <note>original: "adjuration"; the act of charging or entreating solemnly, often under oath</note> and expelled. But how can it be proved that the "father of lies" may be bound and forced—through a command and adjuration in the name and power of God—to tell the truth? And what authority <note>original: "warrant"</note> do we have to learn any truth from his mouth? As if to say, "We command you in the name of..."

[Exorcist speaking to the demon]: "God commands that you tell us who sent you. Who sent you? Who sent you?"
"Mother Joan, Mother Joan," he says. 
"Also, we command you to tell us, who sent you?" 
"L. B. conjured me in here," he says.

*Dan.* All this supports what I claim. The Lord gives him power to possess a person. Under a pretext <note>original: "under a colour"; a false appearance or deceptive excuse</note>, he will act as if he were sent by a conjurer or by a witch. One thinks the devil enters at her request; the other supposes he actually binds the devil to the task, whereas he rules both their minds and sets them to work. Then he willingly exposes <term>original: "bewray"; to reveal or betray a secret</term> them, for many subtle purposes, but chiefly so that he may establish conjurations, witchcrafts, and charms. He wants to be sought after, and he wants to set the people to work in their troubles to be worried about witches and conjurers—as though they could cause plagues—and never look to God. By exposing some witches and conjurers, he may gain credit and be believed, even when he accuses falsely, so that he may bring innocent blood upon the land. Let all men take care how they give a verdict upon their oath—especially concerning a person's life—based on his word, no matter how much he seems to be forced into it; everything in him is the deepest craft and subtlety.

# WITCHES AND WITCHCRAFTS

*Sam.* Please, let me speak for a moment. You say the devil willingly exposes <term>original: "bewrayeth"; to reveal or betray a secret</term> witches and conjurers, and that he does so for many subtle purposes. I have heard of several things done recently that seem quite the opposite, where it appears he takes it very badly when they confess and reveal matters.

*Dan.* He will pretend to be offended by it, but let us hear the details, and you shall see plainly that he is performing a trick <note>original: "juggleth"; deceptive behavior or sleight of hand</note> and making a show of something that is contrary to his actual practice.

*Sam.* Well, I have heard from a very reliable source that a woman recently suspected another woman of being a witch, and believed that the woman had hurt her in some way. She convinced a gentleman to send for the suspected woman. After accusing her in his presence, she left the woman with the gentleman. Taking her aside and walking alone with her, he began to warn and persuade her to renounce the devil and to forsake such wicked ways. While he was thus persuading her, and she was stubbornly denying that she was any such woman, suddenly a weasel or a lobster <note>original: "lobsterre"; in this context, it refers to a "langoust" or "loppester," a regional name for a stoat or similar crawling vermin, not the sea creature</note> appeared some distance from them, looking right at them. "Look," said the gentleman, "that same creature is your spirit." "Ah, master," she said, "that is just a piece of vermin; there are many of them everywhere." Well, as they walked towards it, it disappeared from sight. A moment later, it appeared again and looked at them. "Surely," said the gentleman, "it is your spirit." But she still denied it, and with that, her mouth was twisted to one side <note>original: "drawne awrie"; a physical convulsion often interpreted as a sign of demonic presence</note>. Then he pressed her further, and she confessed everything. She confessed she had hurt and killed people by sending her spirit.

<vocab>witches, conjurers, bewrayeth, divell, weasill, lobsterre, vermine, spirit, familiar</vocab>

<summary>Sam shares a story about a suspected witch who was confronted by a gentleman and confessed after her familiar spirit, appearing as a small animal, was spotted. Daniel argues that such "forced" confessions are actually another form of the devil's deception.</summary>
<keywords>witchcraft, confession, familiar spirits, weasel, deception, George Gifford, spiritual warfare</keywords>

Since the gentleman was not a magistrate <note>original: "justice"; a Justice of the Peace or local official with legal authority</note>, he let her go home, but intended to report the matter to a justice. When she arrived home, another witch met her and said, "Ah, you beast! What have you done? You have betrayed <term>original: "bewrayed"; revealed or exposed a secret</term> us all!" 

"What remedy is there now?" she asked. 

"What remedy?" said the other. "Send your spirit and touch him." 

She sent her spirit, and suddenly the gentleman felt as if a flash of fire was all around him. He lifted up his heart to God and felt no harm. The spirit returned and told her he could not hurt him because he had faith. 

"What then?" said the other witch. "Has he nothing that you may touch?" 

"He has a child," said the first witch. 

"Send your spirit," said the other, "and touch the child." 

She sent her spirit; the child fell into great pain and died. The witches were hanged and confessed.

*Dan.* What is the main point you are trying to make with this story?

*Sam.* To show how unwilling the devil was for the witch to confess and reveal <term>original: "bewray"</term> secrets. No doubt it seems that when the gentleman was talking with her, the devil appeared to call her away for fear she might confess; and when she would not come away, he pulled her mouth askew. When she had confessed, the devil complained to the other witch and made her scold her.

*Dan.* The matter is as clear as can be: he willingly exposed them. Would you imagine the contrary? Why did he appear in a visible form if not to force her to confess, both by shaming <note>original: "abashing"</note> her and by giving the gentleman obvious proof? Especially when...

**Sam.** I will tell you another thing that happened recently. A woman who was suspected of being a witch and of having caused some harm among the cattle was examined. She confessed that she did indeed have a spirit which lived in a hollow tree. There was a hole in the tree from which he spoke to her. Whenever she was offended by anyone, she went to that tree and sent him to kill their cattle. 

She was persuaded to confess her fault openly and to promise that she would utterly forsake such ungodly ways. After she had made this open confession, the spirit came to her while she was alone. "Ah," he said, "you have confessed and betrayed <term>original: "bewrayed"; revealed a secret or person</term> everything! I have half a mind <note>original: "could teeme it"; to be inclined or disposed to do something</note> to tear you to pieces!" At that, she was afraid; she twisted away <note>original: "wound away"</note> and escaped into the company of others. 

Within a few weeks after that, she became very angry with a certain man. She went toward the tree, and before she even reached it, the spirit said, "Ah, why have you come? Who has angered you?" 

"Such-and-such a man," said the witch. 

"And what would you have me do?" asked the spirit. 

"He has," she said, "two horses..."

*Dan.* You must consider that the devil deludes witches in many ways and makes them believe things that are not true at all. In *Germany* and other countries, the devils have so deluded the witches as to make them believe that they raise storms <note>original: "tempests"</note> of lightning and thunder. For the devils know when these things are coming—storms of wind and thunder—and they would gladly <note>original: "fain"</note> make the blind world believe that those great works of God are not God's, but the devil's; that is why he covets to appear in them. These devils make the witches believe that, at their request, they kill both men and animals and cause many kinds of affliction. In reality, many of these things happen naturally, though the devils make it seem as if they caused them. In the remaining cases where they *do* have the power to act, they provoke the witch to "send" them as a mere pretext <note>original: "under a color"; an appearance or pretense</note>. These devils make the witches in some places believe that they are turned into the likeness of wolves, that they tear and rip apart sheep, that they meet together and feast, and that sometimes they fly or ride through the air. In truth, these things are not so at all, but are strong delusions in the witches' imaginations <note>original: "fantasies"</note>. In the same way, the devil can delude a poor woman by appearing in the likeness of another woman delivering a mouse or a cat to her. Or he can plant a strong delusion in a mind oppressed with melancholy <note>original: "melancholie"; in early medicine, this was an excess of "black bile" thought to cause depression, paranoia, and hallucinations</note>, making them believe something happened which never actually did. Men must be wise in these matters, or else they may easily be outmaneuvered <note>original: "circumvented"</note> by the crafts of *Satan* and drawn into great sin.

**M. B.** If it is as you say, then how should a jury condemn any witch by their verdict? For she has not killed anyone, nor has the devil done so at her request; rather, he makes her believe he did it at her request.

**Daniel:** According to the word of God, a witch ought to be put to death. This is not because she kills people—for she cannot do that (unless she is one of those witches who kill by poison, which they either receive from the devil or he teaches them how to make)—but because she deals with devils. Therefore, if a jury finds proof that she has dealt with devils, they may and should find her guilty of witchcraft.

**M. B.** If they find them guilty of dealing with devils, but cannot prove they have murdered anyone, the law does not put them to death.

**Daniel:** It is to be wished that the law were more perfect in that respect, so as to eliminate all such abominations. These "cunning men" <note>original: "cunning men"; practitioners of folk magic or "white" witchcraft who were often consulted to find lost items or cure ailments</note> and women who deal with spirits and charms—appearing to do good while leading the people into many kinds of wickedness <note>original: "impieties"</note>—along with all others who have familiarity with devils or use conjurations <note>original: "conjurations"; rituals used to summon or command spirits</note>, ought to be rooted out, so that others might see it and be afraid.

**M. B.** You will not allow the testimony of devils to have any credit with a jury. What, then, do you say regarding the testimony of men? There are some who die and, on their deathbeds, insist that they are bewitched, claiming precisely that this or that person has done it. This falls under that other point regarding "likelihoods" <note>original: "likelihoods"; circumstantial evidence or probable signs used in legal reasoning</note>.

**Daniel:** They are indeed bewitched, for the devil...

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page continues Daniel’s explanation of how Satan orchestrates the illusion of witchcraft. He argues that the devil manipulates natural events and human psychology to make people believe in the power of witches, even when none exists.</meta>

<page-num>97</page-num>
<header>WITCHES AND WITCHCRAFTS.</header>
<sig>H</sig>

...bewitch. In those events involving sickness, lameness, and death that arise from natural causes, he <note>Satan</note> works in such a way that he makes the witch believe she is responsible for them. He desires this to come to light through her confession <note>George Gifford often argued that "witches" were actually deluded by the devil into thinking they had performed magic they hadn't actually done</note>. Now, in cases where he has no witch to work through, he gains a great advantage if he can create a strong suspicion of any man or woman in someone's mind. For once it has begun, he pursues it with all his power and cunning. 

If someone is afflicted with the painful torment of sickness and is so ignorant and lacking in faith in God’s providence <note>original: "providence"; the belief in God’s protective care and intervention in the world</note> that they imagine the devil does it because a witch sent him, the devil will deceive them and make them believe that the witch is standing right beside them. The suspected man or woman cannot actually be there; who then creates that illusion but Satan? Another person is afraid that the devil will be sent to them by the person they suspect is a witch. Thus, through a lack of faith in God, they give the devil more power over them, either to cause harm or to appear to them. 

For Satan haunts all people continually, seeking every opportunity, and does not need to be sent by any man or woman. Those who reason this way are extremely blind: "An evil spirit came and appeared to me after I had angered a certain woman; therefore, she must have sent him." If Satan has the power to do harm, or knows that something bad is about to happen, is he not clever enough to make the person who will suffer the harm argue with someone they might suspect? That way, the harm seems to come from that person. Furthermore, in fear and in the dark, people take some little cat...

<summary>Daniel explains that Satan manipulates natural misfortunes and human suspicion to create the false impression that witches have supernatural power. He argues that the devil does not need to be commanded by humans but acts independently to exploit those who lack faith in God’s providence.</summary>

If the devil knows that harm has come to men or animals through natural causes, he works either to make people offended and pick a fight with the person who is suspected, or at least to convince them of such hostility that the harm seems to have come from that person. If he actually causes torment by possessing a body, he will not hesitate to lie and say that a certain woman sent him.

*M. B.* And does it not sometimes happen that, just as the devil says a woman sent him, the woman confesses as much during her interrogation?

*Dan.* Yes, but I am speaking of cases where he has no witch to work through, but instead harasses innocent people with suspicion after suspicion so that men may become guilty of shedding innocent blood. He tells the truth sometimes so that he will be believed when he lies. No one should be so foolish as to think that he will ever tell the truth except for some evil purpose.

*M. B.* Yet this seems the strangest of all to me: how so many things could happen, as it...

*M. B.* It happens sometimes that, based on suspicion and common reputation <note>original: "common fame"</note>, the jury is correct, and the person who would not confess to any witchcraft during examination or trial <note>original: "arraignment"</note>, confesses to it once condemned.

*Dan.* Granting that a jury—acting on Satan's testimony, or suspicions and common reputation—sometimes happens to be right (which I fear is very rare), that still provides no justification before God for men who have taken an oath. For are they sworn to indict based on likelihoods, or based on knowledge which they shall find through sound testimony or confession? If the suspected person is indeed a witch, yet there is no proof, the jury acts more rightly in acquitting than in condemning. For what authority do they have, under their oath, to rely on guesswork, or to declare something as fact which they do not know to be true?

*M. B.* I agree that men sin <note>original: "offend"</note> grievously if they condemn the innocent upon insufficient proof, especially because they are solemnly sworn. But if they are correct, even if it is only by conjectures and likelihoods, I cannot see how they have done wrong in that instance: they do not condemn the innocent, and they do the party no wrong.

<summary>Daniel argues that juries should never convict based on suspicions or "devil's testimony," asserting that it is better to let a witch go free than to violate a legal oath by guessing. She was angry with him because she wanted to borrow five shillings from him, and he refused to lend it to her. The woman also took an oath, stating that she believed in her conscience that the old woman was a witch and that she had killed her husband.

*Dan.* If witnesses do swear directly that, to their knowledge, a matter was such and such, and they swear falsely, the jury is blameless <note>original: "cleere"; meaning the jury is not legally or morally responsible for a wrong verdict if they acted in good faith based on the testimony provided.</note> if they proceed according to the evidence—unless the jury perceives that their oath cannot be true. But how does that make the testimony sufficient when men only *think* something is true, and can show no necessary reason to ground their thoughts upon? For example, let us look at all these cases: which one could prove that she must necessarily be a witch? One says her husband swore upon his death <note>original: "tooke it upon his death"; a formal deathbed oath, then considered powerful evidence.</note> that she killed him because he would not lend her five shillings. Does this prove she bewitched him? Can the devil kill a man at his pleasure, just to gratify the witch? Is it not rather to be judged that he died of some wasting illness <note>original: "pining sicknesse"</note> resulting from a poor physical constitution <note>original: "evill constitution of bodie"</note>? The devil would know of this illness and would set the man at odds with some old woman or another, so that it might breed suspicion of witchcraft.

*Sam.* You see, there were some things which could not be done except by the devil.

*Dan.* Indeed, the giant face which the man thought he saw was an illusion of the devil. But is this good proof: the devil appears to a man after he has displeased a woman, therefore she sent him? Does Satan not haunt all men continually? And would he not—if he could get permission from God—terrify them with such illusions, especially when men are afraid and have strong imaginations? What reason did the woman show—the one who swore on her conscience <note>original: "toke it upon her conscience"</note> that the old woman killed her child—to prove that it was so? If she thought so...

<header>A DIALOGUE CONCERNING</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

...another woman sent him? He cannot turn himself into any likeness unless God gives him permission, just as He justly permits it so that he may delude ignorant people. No witch can give him the power to appear to anyone in a visible shape. He was granted this [ability to appear] by God, and Satan will immediately stir up anger and then appear, so that it might seem as if his appearance grew out of that anger.

**Samuel.** We see that he appears to witches and conjurers <note>original: "conjurers"; individuals who supposedly call up spirits through rituals or spells.</note>.

**Daniel.** Yes, but we must not think he can take on a likeness to appear in whenever he pleases. That he appears to witches and conjurers is permitted by God’s wrath, so that he may strongly delude such wicked people who will not hear and obey the voice of the Lord God. For the devils are chained up by God’s most mighty power and providence <note>original: "providence"; the protective care of God or nature as a spiritual power.</note>, and they proceed only as far as He lets out their chain; they cannot go one inch further. Where people love darkness more than light, he is given permission to do many things. Some he terrifies with ugly shapes; some he entices with fair shows <note>original: "shewes"; appearances or spectacles.</note>; others he plays with in the likeness of a weasel, or mouse, or some such small vermin <note>It was a common belief in English folklore that witches were accompanied by "familiars," spirits in the form of small animals.</note>.

**Samuel.** I thought Satan could appear in whatever likeness he wanted, and to whomever he wanted, if the witch sent him.

**Daniel.** You were much deceived in that, for being sent by a witch can give him no power. If he had that power, he would undoubtedly appear to many people in all places, as often and in such a manner as would best serve [his purpose]...

<summary>Daniel argues that the devil’s ability to take physical form is strictly controlled by God’s providence rather than the will of a witch. He explains that demonic appearances are a divine judgment intended to delude those who reject God.</summary>
<keywords>George Gifford, sovereignty of God, demonic possession, familiars, spiritual delusion, providence, witchcraft law</keywords>

--- Page 126 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Daniel and Samuel. Daniel is reinforcing the argument that the devil only operates under God's sovereignty. He specifically addresses the common belief that witches have the power to "send" the devil to do harm, arguing instead that the devil manipulates witches to make them believe they are in control.</meta>

<page-num>111</page-num>
<header>WITCHES AND WITCHCRAFTS.</header>

...his turn. Therefore, if he appears to any man, let that man think, "God has given him permission to go this far with me," and let him call for faith to resist him and for true wisdom so that he may not be deceived or deluded by him.

**Samuel.** But does he not appear sometimes when the witches send him?

**Daniel.** Where he finds it is granted to him to appear, he moves witches to send him, if he has any to work through <note>original: "deale by"; to act through or use as an agent.</note>. But if there are none, he will still appear and act as far as he has been given power.

**Samuel.** But what do you say about the woman’s hens?

**Daniel.** What should one say to them when they are dead?

**Samuel.** I mean, do you not think they were bewitched?

**Daniel.** Christ says a sparrow cannot fall without the will of your heavenly Father <note>A reference to Matthew 10:29.</note>; and is not a hen as good as a sparrow?

**Samuel.** No, I am fully persuaded by what you have said that the devil cannot touch anything to kill or hurt it without special permission from God. They <note>The witches.</note> can give him no power; she thinks she is setting him on, but it is he who sets her to work. Let these things no longer be questioned. But was it not evident that the devil killed those hens? Because after the burning of one hen <note>A common folk-magic remedy; it was believed that burning a bewitched animal would break the spell or force the witch to appear.</note>, the woman had no more that died. If Satan did not do it, how could they stop dying because of that? You said that he, where he has power to cause such physical harm, is willing to stop...

<summary>Daniel explains that the devil only appears or harms creatures like hens with God's permission, often tricking witches into thinking they are the ones in command. Samuel questions the efficacy of folk remedies, like burning a dead hen to stop further deaths in the flock.</summary>

<keywords>sovereignty of God, demonic agency, folk magic, counter-spells, Matthew 10:29, spiritual deception</keywords>

--- Page 127 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Daniel and Samuel. Daniel is systematically dismantling Samuel's "evidence" for witchcraft, arguing that what Samuel sees as proof is actually the devil's manipulation of human perception and timing.</meta>

<page-num>112</page-num>
<header>A DIALOGUE CONCERNING</header>

...that such wickedness may be practiced. And then, if this harm was done by the devil, is it not to be thought that the woman was a witch, seeing it followed after she was angry? Suppose that Satan, having power to do what he did, would be sent by the witch as a cover <note>original: "for a colour"; a pretext or deceptive appearance meant to hide the truth.</note>, and to make it evident, he stirred up anger between her and that other woman, to make men think that he would not act unless persuaded by her being angry. And so we could do no less than find her [guilty of being] a witch.

*Dan.* These are weak foundations to set such a weighty building upon. For first, it is not certain that the devil killed those hens. Might it not be that they had some infection which he knew would kill them, and he craftily brings the matter about, making two women quarrel (which is the easiest matter in a hundred) even as the hens were dying, so that it might seem they were bewitched? 

But you say then, how could it be that upon the burning of a hen <note>A folk remedy where burning a diseased or "bewitched" animal was thought to break the spell.</note>, no more died, if the devil did not kill them? No, how can you tell but that no more would have died anyway, even if the live hen had not been burned? What if he saw there should no more die, and thereupon moved the heart of that woman to use that witchcraft in burning a hen, so that it might seem that was a present remedy to drive away devils? Or suppose <note>original: "put case"</note> he had the power to kill the woman’s hens: either he is a weak killer, or else he goes to his work but lazily. He could kill a great herd of swine quickly when Christ gave him leave <note>Referring to the Miracle of the Gadarene Swine in the Gospels.</note>: could he not, if the woman had five thousand hens, have killed them...

<summary>Daniel argues that what looks like witchcraft is often the devil's opportunistic timing, using natural animal deaths and human quarrels to trick people into performing superstitious rituals like burning hens.</summary>
<keywords>witchcraft, devil's deception, Gadarene swine, burning the hen, superstition, George Gifford, providentialism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Samuel and Daniel. Daniel is mocking the idea that a devil or "familiar" spirit would be deterred from killing livestock simply because a farmer burned a hen as a counter-spell. He argues that these events are coincidental or psychological rather than supernatural.</meta>

<page-num>113</page-num>
<header>WITCHES AND WITCHCRAFTS.</header>

...all at once? Why did the fool then only nibble, killing now one and then another, and was so scared away before he had killed all? If he had power to kill before the hen was burned, why did he not kill the rest then, when they went about to burn a hen <note>A folk remedy or "white magic" practice where burning a diseased animal was thought to break a witch's spell or drive away her familiar.</note>? It may be he did not know what they were doing; he was laid softly in his pot of wool <note>original: "pot of wooll"; imps or "familiars" were often believed to be kept by witches in wool-lined pots or boxes.</note>. And coming to kill another hen, he was met with it, smelled the roast meat, and was scared.

**Sam.** Then you think he did not kill those hens.

**Dan.** What certainty had you that he did kill them? You reached a verdict upon your oath <note>original: "found it upon your oath"; as a member of a jury, Samuel has sworn that the evidence proved the witch’s guilt.</note> that he killed them, and that such a woman sent him and set him to work; and yet it is a hundred to one he never had the power to touch them.

**Sam.** But what can you say to the other case: the man who could not help but kiss under his cow's tail?

**Dan.** I say he was far in love with his cow. Let such men learn to know God and to expel fantasies <note>original: "fantasies"; here meaning delusions, hallucinations, or obsessive thoughts.</note> out of their minds, so that the devil may not have such power over them. For he works in the fantasies of a man's mind, and more strongly where they fear him, as it appears this man did. Satan worked many foolish imaginations in this man’s mind; and to make him believe he was bewitched, he makes him fall out with someone who may be suspected. And thus you jurymen take your oath and condemn many innocent persons because you believe the devil, and imagine that witches do what they cannot do.

**M. B.** I have heard of many that have been con—

<sig>I</sig>

<summary>Daniel critiques the logic of witch trials, arguing that the strange behaviors attributed to witchcraft are actually the result of the devil manipulating human "fantasies" and fears. He warns that jurymen are condemning innocent people by believing these demonic delusions and coincidences.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the conversation between Daniel, Samuel, and a neighbor (M.B.). Daniel has been arguing that the fear of witches distracts from the sovereignty of God and that many people accused of witchcraft are convicted on flimsy evidence or even when innocent.</meta>

<page-num>114</page-num>
<header>A DIALOGUE CONCERNING</header>

...condemned as witches who have maintained until their deaths that they were innocent. And many of them have had far weaker evidence brought against them than those that have been mentioned.

*Dan.* Yes, that is what I am saying: men consider the high sovereignty and providence of God over all things so little. They attribute so much to the power of the devil and to the anger of witches, and are in such fear of them, that the slightest appearance made by the tricks <note>original: "sleights"; meaning deceits or crafty schemes.</note> of Satan deceives them. The only way for men who wish to avoid the snares and subtleties of the devil and all harm from him is this: to hear the voice of God, to be taught by Him through His living word, which is full of pure light to reveal and expel the dark mists of Satan, in which he leads men astray. And they must be armed with faith to resist him, as the holy Apostle St. Peter instructs. For those who forsake this path are led astray into gross errors and many abominable sins, which lead men to destruction. I must now bid you farewell.

*M. B.* I would be happy to hear more on these matters; I see how foolishly <note>original: "fondly"; in this period, it meant foolishly or naively rather than affectionately.</note> I have erred. But since you must go, I hope we shall meet here again at some other time; God keep you!

*Sam.* I am bound to give you great thanks. And I pray you, when the opportunity arises and you come this way, let us see you at my house.

*M. B.* I did not think there were such subtle practices of the devil, nor such great sins as he leads men into.

<summary>Daniel concludes the dialogue by exhorting his companions to trust in God’s providence and scripture rather than fearing the devil’s tricks, prompting M.B. and Samuel to admit their previous ignorance and superstitions regarding witchcraft.</summary>

*Sam.* It is strange to see how many thousands are led astray and deceived—even many who are very wise men.

*M. B.* The devil is too crafty for the wisest, unless they have the light of God’s word.

*The wife of Sam.* Husband, over there comes Goodwife <note>original: "good wife"; a title for a woman of humble but respectable social standing, the female equivalent of "Goodman."</note> R.

*Sam.* I wish she had come sooner.

*The good wife R.* Hello! Who is inside, if I may come in?

*The wife of Sam.* I wish you had come a little sooner; there was someone here just now who said you are a witch.

*The good wife R.* Did someone say I am a witch? You are only joking.

*The wife of Sam.* No, I assure you he was in dead earnest.

*The good wife R.* Me, a witch? I defy whoever says it, even if he were a lord. I wish all the witches in the land were hanged—and their spirits right along with them!

*M. B.* Wouldn't you be happy, if their spirits were hanged up with them, to have a gown lined <note>original: "furred"; meaning trimmed or lined with fur for warmth and status.</note> with some of their skins?

*The good wife R.* Fie on them <note>original: "Out upon them"; an exclamation used to express strong disapproval or abhorrence.</note>! That would be some fur indeed!

*Sam.* Wife, why did you say that he said Goodwife R. is a witch? He did not say that.

*The wife of Sam.* Husband, I paid close enough attention to his words; he said she is a witch.

*Sam.* He does not know her; how could he say she is a witch?

<summary>A local woman named Goodwife R. enters the conversation and is shocked to learn she has been accused of being a witch, prompting a defensive and angry response. The scene illustrates how quickly rumors of witchcraft could spread and cause social friction among neighbors.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the conversation between Samuel, his wife, the skeptic M. B., and their neighbor Goodwife R. The discussion centers on folk remedies and "counter-magic" used to solve household problems like failed butter-making, which many people at the time attributed to witchcraft rather than natural causes.</meta>

<page-num>116</page-num>

***The wife of Sam.*** What if he didn't know her? Didn’t he say she acted like a witch when she heated the spit <note>original: "spit"; a long, thin metal rod used for roasting meat over a fire. Heating it was a common folk ritual to "burn" a witch by proxy.</note> red hot and thrust it into her cream when the butter wouldn't come <note>original: "come"; the process of cream thickening into butter during churning.</note>?

***Sam.*** Indeed, wife, you say the truth. He said that was a thing taught by the devil, as was also the burning of a hen or a hog alive, and all such similar tricks.

***The good wife R.*** Is that witchcraft? Some "Scripture man" <note>original: "Scripture man"; a dismissive term for someone who relies strictly on the Bible and rejects traditional folk magic and "cunning" remedies.</note> has told you so. Did the devil teach it? No, the good woman at R. H. taught it to my husband. She does more good in one year than all these "Scripture men" will do as long as they live.

***M. B.*** Who do you think taught it to the cunning woman <note>original: "cunning woman"; a practitioner of "white" magic or folk medicine, often sought out to break spells or heal the sick.</note> at R. H.?

***The good wife R.*** It is a gift that God has given her. I think the Holy Spirit of God teaches her.

***M. B.*** So you don't think, then, that the devil teaches her?

***The good wife R.*** Why should I think that the devil teaches her? Did you ever hear that the devil taught anyone a good thing?

***M. B.*** Do you know for certain that it was a good thing?

***The good wife R.*** Wasn't it a good thing to drive the evil spirit out of my cream?

***M. B.*** Do you think the devil was afraid of your roasting spit?

***The good wife R.*** I know he was driven away, and we have been rid of him ever since.

***M. B.*** Can a metal spit hurt him?

***The good wife R.*** It does hurt him, or it hurts the witch—one of them, I am sure—for he comes no...

<meta>catchword: no</meta>
