B.** It is certain that spirits cannot be hurt except with spiritual weapons; therefore, your spit <note>original: "spit"; a metal rod used for roasting meat.</note> cannot frighten <note>original: "fray"; to scare or drive away.</note> or hurt the devil. And how can it hurt the witch? You didn't think she was actually in your cream, did you?

*The good wife R.* Some think she is there, and therefore when they thrust in the spit they say: "If you are here, have at your eye!" <note>A ritualistic threat intended to physically harm the witch by attacking her "presence" in the cream.</note>

**M. B.** If she were in your cream, your butter was not very clean.

*The good wife R.* You are in a joking mood, M. B. I know you are of my mind, even though you put these questions to me. For I am sure no one has counseled people to go to the cunning folk <note>original: "cunning folk"; practitioners of folk magic or "white" witches sought out for help against spells.</note> more than you have.

**M. B.** I was of your mind, but I am not now, for I see how foolish I was. I am sorry that I ever offended so seriously as to counsel anyone to seek help from devils.

*The good wife R.* Why, M. B., who has schooled you today? I am sure you were of another mind no longer ago than yesterday.

*The wife of Sam.* Truly, Goodwife R., I think my husband has changed his mind <note>original: "turned"; converted or changed his opinion.</note> also; there has been someone here reasoning with them for three or four hours.

*The good wife R.* Is your husband changed too? I wish you would lose all your hens one after another, and then I wish she would set her spirit upon your ducks and your geese, and leave you not one alive. Will you come to defend witches?

B.*** I would seek help; I would carry him to the blacksmith <note>original: "smith"</note> to search if he were not pricked <note>Injured by a nail during shoeing.</note> or graveling <note>original: "graveld"; a condition where grit gets trapped between the hoof and the shoe, causing lameness.</note>.

***M. B.*** You think the devil can kill men's livestock <note>original: "cattell"; in this period, it referred to all farm animals, not just cows.</note>, and lame both man and beast at his pleasure: you think if the witch begs him and sends him he will go, and if she does not want him to go, he will not interfere. And you think when he does come, you can drive him away with a hot spit, or by burning a live hen or a pig.

***The good wife R.*** Don't tell me I "think" so, for you yourself have thought so: and let them say what they can, all the theologians <note>original: "Scripture men"; those who study or cite the Bible as their primary authority.</note> in the world shall never persuade me otherwise.

***M. B.*** I do wonder, not so much at your ignorance, but at the fact that I was once of the same mind that you are, and could not see my own folly.

***The good wife R.*** Folly? How wise you have become all of a sudden! I know that their spirits lie lurking, for they nurture <note>original: "foster"</note> them: and when anyone has angered them, then they call them forth and send them. And look—

### WITCHES AND WITCHCRAFTS.

Whatever they command them to do, or hire them to do, that shall be done: as when she is angry, the spirit will ask her, "What shall I do?" "Such a man has mistreated <note>original: "misused"</note> me," she says; "Go kill his cow." Right away <note>original: "by and by"; in this period, the phrase meant "immediately."</note>, he goes and does it. "Go kill such a woman's hens." Down they go. And some of them are not content to do these lesser harms, but they will say, "Go make such a man lame, kill him, or kill his child." Then they are ready and will do anything; and I think they are fortunate <note>original: "happie"</note> who can learn how to drive them away.

*M. B.* If I should reason with you out of the Word of God <note>The Bible.</note>, you would see that all this which you say is false. The devil <note>original: "divell"</note> cannot kill nor hurt anything, no, not so much as a poor hen. If he had such power, who could escape him? Would he wait <note>original: "tarrie"</note> to be sent or pleaded with <note>original: "intreated"</note> by a woman? He is an instigator <note>original: "stirrer up"</note> of all harms and mischiefs.

*The good wife R.* Why do you tell me of God’s Word? Does not God's Word say there are witches, and do you not think God allows <note>original: "suffer"</note> bad people? Are you a turncoat? <note>original: "turne coate"; a person who deserts one party or cause to join an opposing one.</note> Farewell, I will no longer talk with you.

*M. B.* She is stubborn <note>original: "wilfull"</note> indeed. I will leave you also.

*Sam.* I thank you for your good company.

# The Percy Society,

->FOR THE<-

## PUBLICATION OF ANCIENT BALLADS, POETRY, AND POPULAR LITERATURE.

---

**At** a General Meeting of the **Percy Society**, held in the Rooms of the **Royal Society of Literature**, on Thursday the 1st of May, 1845—

**The Right Honorable Lord Braybrooke**, President, in the Chair—

The business of the day having been opened with an address by the President,

The Secretary read the Report of the Council, dated the 1st of May, whereupon it was—

*Resolved*—That the Report be received and adopted, and the thanks of the Society be given to the Council for their services.

The Report of the Auditors, dated the 28th of April, was read by the Secretary, whereupon it was—

*Resolved*—That the Report of the Auditors be received and adopted, and that the thanks of the Society be given them for their services.

The Meeting then proceeded to the election of Officers, when—

->**The Right Honorable Lord Braybrooke, Fellow of the Society of Antiquaries** <note>original: "F.S.A."</note><-

was elected President.

<page-num>119</page-num>

->RICHARDS, PRINTER, 100, ST. MARTIN'S LANE.<-

### WITCHES AND WITCHCRAFTS.

Whatever they command them to do, or hire them to do, that shall be done: as when she is angry, the spirit will ask her, "What shall I do?" "Such a man has mistreated <note>original: "misused"</note> me," she says; "Go kill his cow." Right away <note>original: "by and by"; in this period, the phrase meant "immediately."</note>, he goes and does it. "Go kill such a woman's hens." Down they go. And some of them are not content to do these lesser harms, but they will say, "Go make such a man lame, kill him, or kill his child." Then they are ready and will do anything; and I think they are fortunate <note>original: "happie"</note> who can learn how to drive them away.

*M. B.* If I should reason with you from the Word of God <note>The Bible.</note>, you would see that all this which you say is false. The devil <note>original: "divell"</note> cannot kill nor hurt anything, no, not even <note>original: "not so much as"</note> a poor hen. If he had such power, who could escape him? Would he wait <note>original: "tarrie"</note> to be sent or pleaded with <note>original: "intreated"</note> by a woman? He is an instigator <note>original: "stirrer up"</note> of all harms and mischiefs.

*The good wife R.* Why do you tell me of God’s Word? Does not God's Word say there are witches, and do you not think God allows <note>original: "suffer"</note> bad people? Are you a turncoat? <note>original: "turne coate"; a person who deserts one party or cause to join an opposing one.</note> Farewell, I will no longer talk with you.

*M. B.* She is stubborn <note>original: "wilfull"</note> indeed. I will leave...

### ANNUAL REPORT—MAY 1ST, 1845.

The Council of the Percy Society feels satisfaction in presenting to the Society the report of their work during the fifth year of the Society’s existence. At the close of the fourth year, the Society had been run

--- Page 138 ---
<meta>This page continues the Percy Society's fifth annual report, moving from the list of elected officers to an explanation of the Society's financial state and publication output.</meta>

[The Society was] in debt <note>original: "in arrears"; referring here to outstanding financial obligations</note>, mainly because it published significantly more material during that year than its funds could consistently afford. The previous report shows that while only between 1,000 and 1,100 pages were printed in each of the first few years, and 1,359 pages in the second year, the fourth year saw no fewer than 1,550 pages printed. This statement is necessary to explain why the amount of material provided to members during the current year has been less than what was given in the previous year. 

The Council elected to manage the Society’s affairs during its fifth year considered it a primary duty to clear the Society's debt through strict financial discipline, and they are happy to report that these efforts have been successful. They can also state that membership has increased rapidly over the last year. The number of complete sets of the Society’s publications purchased by new members has significantly helped stabilize the Society's current position. Furthermore, the Council is encouraged by the prospect that this growth will continue during the year that is now beginning. Only two or three sets of the first year’s publications remain in stock; once those are sold, complete sets of the Percy Society <note>The Percy Society (1840–1852) was a British text publication society that focused on printing rare early English poems, ballads, and popular literature.</note> publications will become rare and even more valuable.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Among other works currently in various stages of preparation, the Council expects the following to be ready for distribution during the coming year:

The Council wishes to repeat the invitation made in its former reports to members of the Society and others to suggest new works for consideration. The Society is grateful to all gentlemen who may contribute rare tracts or ballads from private collections, as well as to the different editors whose zeal and unpaid labor allow these works to be introduced to the world. The thanks of the Society are especially due to the University of Cambridge for the kindness with which the manuscript of the *Seven Sages* was placed at the disposal of the editor of the text published during the present year, and for the permission to print from it.

# REPORT OF THE AUDITORS

We, the auditors appointed by the Council of the Percy Society <note>The Percy Society (1840–1852) was a book club dedicated to publishing rare old English poems and traditional ballads.</note> to examine the accounts of the treasurer from May 21, 1844, to April 28, 1845, certify that the treasurer has presented his accounts to us. We have thoroughly examined them, along with his receipts and other vouchers, and find them to be perfectly correct and satisfactory.

Furthermore, we report that the following is a correct summary of the receipts and expenditures of the Society during the period mentioned:

| RECEIPTS | £ | s. | d. | EXPENDITURE | £ | s. | d. |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| Subscriptions paid for the year ending May 1, 1845 | 198 | 0 | 0 | To Mr. Richards for printing | 239 | 18 | 0 |
| Arrears <note>Overdue subscription payments from previous years.</note> received during the year | 42 | 0 | 0 | Messrs. Fuller and Thornhill for paper | 58 | 11 | 5 |
| Sets of publications from the first four years purchased by twenty-three new members | 92 | 0 | 0 | For transcripts <note>Handwritten copies of ancient manuscripts made so that the printers had a text to work from.</note> | 28 | 10 | 6 |
| Balance paid over by the former treasurer | 28 | 10 | 6 | For binding | 14 | 5 | 0 |
| Subscriptions for the year beginning May 1, 1845 | 19 | 0 | 0 | Expenses allowed to agents | 1 | 1 | 3 |
| | | | | Advertising the 1844 General Meeting | 3 | 13 | 6 |
| | | | | Petty expenses, postage, etc. | 9 | 4 | 6 |
| | | | | Publications from the first year bought to complete sets | 0 | 9 | 0 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| | | | | **Total Expenditure** | 355 | 13 | 2 |
| | | | | Balance remaining in hand | 23 | 17 | 4 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Total** | **£379** | **10** | **6** | **Total** | **£379** | **10** | **6** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |

We also certify that the sum of £18 0s. 5d., which was part of the total paid for transcripts, has been paid toward the expenses of the upcoming year.

Additionally, the treasurer has reported to us that a number of subscriptions for the past year remain unpaid; however, he confidently expects that these will be paid soon.

-> (Signed) { **T. CROFTON CROKER.** <note>Thomas Crofton Croker (1798–1854) was an Irish antiquary best known for his collections of Irish folklore.</note> <-
-> **JOHN BLACHFORD.** <-

# The Percy Society,

->FOR THE<-

## PUBLICATION OF ANCIENT BALLADS, POETRY, AND POPULAR LITERATURE.

---

### Council, 1845–1846.

**THE RIGHT HONORABLE LORD BRAYBROOKE, F.S.A. <note>F.S.A.: Fellow of the Society of Antiquaries, a prestigious group of historians and archaeologists.</note> PRESIDENT.**

**T. AMYOT, ESQ. F.R.S. <note>F.R.S.: Fellow of the Royal Society, a scientific academy.</note> TREASURER OF THE SOCIETY OF ANTIQUARIES.**
**W. H. BLACK, ESQ.**
**W. CHAPPELL, ESQ. F.S.A.**
**J. PAYNE COLLIER, ESQ. F.S.A.**
**BOLTON CORNEY, ESQ.**
**PETER CUNNINGHAM, ESQ.**
**HENRY J. DIXON, ESQ.**
**FREDERICK W. FAIRHOLT, ESQ. F.S.A.**
**J. O. HALLIWELL, ESQ. F.R.S. F.S.A.**

<column-break/>

**W. JERDAN, ESQ. F.S.A., M.R.S.L. <note>M.R.S.L.: Member of the Royal Society of Literature.</note>**
**CAPTAIN JOHNS, ROYAL MARINES <note>original: "R.M."</note>**
**T. J. PETTIGREW, ESQ. F.R.S., F.S.A.**
**WILLIAM SANDYS, ESQ. F.S.A.**
**W. J. THOMS, ESQ. F.S.A.**
**T. WRIGHT, ESQ. M.A., F.S.A., SECRETARY AND TREASURER.**

---

->*Subscription £1 per year <note>original: "Annum"</note>.*<-

---

A new work is issued on the first day of every other month and will be delivered to the order of each member at Mr. Richards’s printing office, 100 St. Martin’s Lane, where subscriptions are also received.

Subscriptions become due in advance on the 1st of May each year, and no books are considered owed until the subscription has been paid.

The Society is limited to five hundred members.

Persons wishing to become members are requested to send their names to the SECRETARY, in care of Mr. Richards, 100 St. Martin’s Lane, London, where all communications regarding the affairs of the Society should be addressed.

---

->*New members may obtain the works already printed by paying the subscription for those years. Very few copies remain of the publications from the first year.*<-

# Works Already Printed.

## FIRST YEAR.

**A Collection of Old Ballads before <note>original: "anterior to"</note> the Reign of Charles I.**
By John Skelton, Stephen Peel, Churchyard, Tarlton, Elderton, Deloney, etc. etc. Edited by J. Payne Collier, Esq. F.S.A.

**A Search for Money;**
Or the Lamentable Complaint for the loss of the Wandering Knight Monsieur Money <note>original: "Mounsieur l’Argent"</note>;" containing curious topographical details of London and its suburbs. By William Rowley. 1609. Reprinted from the only known copy. Edited by J. Payne Collier, Esq. F.S.A.

**The Pain and Sorrow of Evil Marriage <note>original: "The Payne and Sorowe of Evyll Maryage"</note>,**
in verse. From a copy believed to be unique, printed by Wynkyn de Worde; with an introduction regarding other works of the same class, and from the same press, by J. Payne Collier, Esq. F.S.A.

**A Selection from the Minor Poems of Dan <note>Dan: An archaic title of honor, derived from the Latin "dominus," used similarly to "Master" or "Sir."</note> John Lydgate.**
Edited by James Orchard Halliwell, Esq. F.R.S., F.S.A., and English Correspondent of the Royal Historical Commission of France.

**The King and a Poor Northern Man.**
Full of "simple mirth and merry plain jests." By Martin Parker. In verse. 1640. Edited by J. Payne Collier, Esq. F.S.A.

**The Revolution in Ireland of 1688,**
Illustrated by the popular ballads of the period. Edited, with introductions and notes, by T. Crofton Croker, Esq. F.S.A., M.R.I.A. <note>M.R.I.A.: Member of the Royal Irish Academy.</note>

**Songs of the London Apprentices <note>original: "Prentices"</note> and Trades,**
During the reigns of Henry VIII, Elizabeth, and James I. Edited by Charles Mackay, Esq.

**The Early Naval Ballads of England.**
Edited by James Orchard Halliwell, Esq. F.R.S.

**Robin Good-fellow; His Mad Pranks and Merry Jests.**
In prose and verse. 1628. With an introduction by J. Payne Collier, Esq. F.S.A.

# The Pleasant History of the Two Angry Women of Abington.
With the humorous mirth of Dick Coomes and Nicholas Proverbs, two serving-men. As it was lately played by the Lord High Admiral’s servants. Written by Henry Porter. 1599. Edited by the Reverend A. Dyce.

# The Book of Courtesy;
An English poem illustrative of the domestic manners of the fifteenth century. Edited by J. O. Halliwell, Esq., F.R.S., etc.

# Kind-Heart’s Dream.
Containing five apparitions, with their invectives <note>original: "Invectives" — harsh or critical speeches.</note> against reigning abuses. By Henry Chettle. Containing <note>Probably a typographical error for "Containing" in the original text.</note> notices of Shakespeare, Nash, etc. A curious picture of the manners and customs of the time. With a life of the author. Edited by E. F. Rimbault, Esq., F.S.A. <note>F.S.A.: Fellow of the Society of Antiquaries.</note>

# The Meeting of Gallants at an Ordinary.
Or the Walks in Paul's <note>original: "Powles" — referring to the interior of St. Paul's Cathedral, which was a common social meeting place.</note>. 1604. Illustrative of manners and customs of the time. Edited by J. O. Halliwell, Esq., F.R.S. <note>Ordinary: A tavern or eating house that served regular meals at a fixed price.</note>

# Old Christmas Carols,
Chiefly taken from manuscript sources. Edited by Thomas Wright, Esq., M.A., F.S.A.

# The Nursery Rhymes of England,
Arranged in classes, with an historical introduction. Edited by J. O. Halliwell, Esq.

# The Pleasant and Sweet History of Patient Grisel <note>original: "Grissel"</note>.
In prose and verse. With an introduction concerning the origin of the story, and its application in various countries. Edited by J. Payne Collier, Esq., F.S.A.

# Specimens of Lyric Poetry Written in England During the Reign of Edward I.
Edited by T. Wright, Esq., M.A., F.S.A.

# A Marriage Triumph.
Solemnized in an epithalamium <note>original: "Epithalamium" — a poem or song written specifically for a bride on her way to her marital chamber.</note> in memory of the happy nuptials between the Count Palatine and the Lady Elizabeth. Written by Thomas Heywood. In verse; 1613. With an introduction, giving an account of other poems by different authors on the same event, by J. Payne Collier, Esq., F.S.A.

# A Knight’s Conjuring, Done in Earnest, Discovered in Jest.
Written in answer to Nash’s *Pierce Penniless*, and containing numerous allusions to manners and customs in London. By Thomas Dekker, 1607. Edited, with a life of the author, by Edward F. Rimbault, Esq., F.S.A.

In English meter (in stanzas) of the fifteenth century; presumed to be the production of a Lollard <note>Lollard: A follower of the religious reformer John Wycliffe; they were precursors to the Protestant Reformation.</note>. Edited by W. H. Black, Esq., Assistant Keeper of the Public Records.

A collection of songs and ballads, chiefly historical, by Richard Johnson, author of "The Seven Champions of Christendom." Reprinted from the edition of 1612. Edited by W. Chappell, Esq., F.S.A.

# A Dialogue of Witches and Witchcraft.
By George Gifford, 1603. Edited by T. Wright, Esq., M.A., F.S.A. <note>F.S.A.: Fellow of the Society of Antiquaries.</note> A curious illustration of the popular superstitions of the latter part of the sixteenth century.

Or Satires and Satirical Epigrams, by Henry Hutton <note>original: "Huttou"</note> of Durham, 1619. # Five Poetical Tracts of the Sixteenth Century.
From unique copies, namely <note>original: "viz." — an abbreviation of the Latin "videlicet"</note>: "The Doctrinal <note>original: "Doctrynall"</note> of Good Servants." "The Book of Maid <note>original: "Mayd"</note> Emlyn." "The New Nut-brown <note>original: "Notborune"</note> Maid." # A Collection of Stories Originally in Latin <note>original: "Latin Stories"</note>,
Illustrative of the history of fiction during the Middle Ages. From manuscripts <note>original: "MSS."</note> of the thirteenth and fourteenth centuries. Edited by T. Wright, Esq., M.A., F.S.A.

# The Harmony <note>original: "Harmonie"</note> of the Church,
Containing spiritual songs and holy hymns. By Michael Drayton. Edited by the Reverend Alexander Dyce.

# Cock Lorel's Boat <note>original: "Cocke Lorell's Bote"</note>,
A satirical poem from a unique copy printed by Wynkyn de Worde <note>A famous early printer and successor to William Caxton.</note>. Edited by E. F. Rimbault, Esq., LL.D. <note>LL.D.: Doctor of Laws.</note>, F.S.A.

# Poems by Sir Henry Wotton.
Edited by the Reverend Alexander Dyce.

# The Harmony of Birds.
A poem, from the only known copy, printed in the middle of the sixteenth century. Edited by J. P. Collier, Esq., F.S.A.

# A Kerry Pastoral.
In imitation of the First Eclogue <note>Eclogue: A short pastoral poem, usually a dialogue between shepherds.</note> of Virgil. Edited by T. Crofton Croker, Esq., F.S.A., M.R.I.A. <note>M.R.I.A.: Member of the Royal Irish Academy.</note>

# FOURTH YEAR.

1597. Edited by Edward F. Rimbault, Esq., LL.D., F.S.A. <note>Bankes's horse, named Morocco, was a famous performing animal in the Elizabethan era, known for "counting" coins and other clever tricks.</note>
